    {
      "definition_anchor": "eq:weighted-path-definitions",
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "eq:weighted-path-definitions",
          "exact_quote": "For a directed simple path $v_0,\\ldots,v_r$, $r\\ge1$, let $e_i=(v_{i-1},v_i)$. At a common time $t$, set $$\\label{eq:weighted-path-definitions}\n \\begin{aligned}\n P_r&=\\prod_{i=1}^r a(h_{v_i\\to v_{i-1}}),\n &Z_r&=\\sum_{i=1}^rD_{e_i},\\\\\n Q_r&=\\mathbb E[(P_r^2+(P'_r)^2)Z_r^2],\n &L_r&=\\mathbb E[(\\Delta h_{v_0})^2P_r^2].\n \\end{aligned}$$ The orientation matters: every factor uses the component at the next vertex excluding the preceding one. Invariance makes these expectations depend only on $r$ and $t$, so we suppress the particular path. Both $P_r$ and $P'_r$ lie in $(0,1]$, and $\\log P_r-\\log P'_r=Z_r$. The mean-value theorem, first for $e^x$ and then for $e^{2x}$, gives $$\\label{eq:product-difference}\n \\mathbb E[(P_r-P'_r)^2]\\le2Q_r,\n \\qquad\n |P_r^2-(P'_r)^2|\n       \\le2(P_r^2+(P'_r)^2)|Z_r|.$$ For example, the derivative of $e^x$ on the segment joining $\\log P_r$ and $\\log P'_r$ is at most $P_r+P'_r$; squaring and using $(P_r+P'_r)^2\\le2(P_r^2+(P'_r)^2)$ proves the first bound. The derivative of $e^{2x}$ on the same segment is at most $2(P_r^2+(P'_r)^2)$, proving the second.",

      "exact_definition": "For a directed simple path $v_0,\\ldots,v_r$, $r\\ge1$, let $e_i=(v_{i-1},v_i)$. Invariance makes these expectations depend only on $r$ and $t$, so we suppress the particular path. Both $P_r$ and $P'_r$ lie in $(0,1]$, and $\\log P_r-\\log P'_r=Z_r$. The mean-value theorem, first for $e^x$ and then for $e^{2x}$, gives $$\\label{eq:product-difference}\n \\mathbb E[(P_r-P'_r)^2]\\le2Q_r,\n \\qquad\n |P_r^2-(P'_r)^2|\n       \\le2(P_r^2+(P'_r)^2)|Z_r|.$$ For example, the derivative of $e^x$ on the segment joining $\\log P_r$ and $\\log P'_r$ is at most $P_r+P'_r$; squaring and using $(P_r+P'_r)^2\\le2(P_r^2+(P'_r)^2)$ proves the first bound. The derivative of $e^{2x}$ on the same segment is at most $2(P_r^2+(P'_r)^2)$, proving the second.",
      "interpretation": "Path product, summed edge discrepancy, and the two weighted second moments used to bound noise and drift.",
      "kind": "definition",

      "definition_anchor": "eq:edge-operator",
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "eq:edge-operator",
          "exact_quote": "A vertex assignment can be read at either endpoint of a directed edge. We compare these two lifts and then isolate the part of an edge assignment orthogonal to both. On the joined assignment spaces define the tail and head lifts $\\mathsf F,\\mathsf H:\\mathcal H_{\\mathrm v}(\\pi)\\to\n\\mathcal H_{\\mathrm e}(\\pi)$, the reversal $\\mathsf J$, and the forward nonbacktracking average $\\mathsf N$ by $$\\begin{aligned}\n (\\mathsf Ff)_{(i,j)}&=f_i,&\n (\\mathsf Hf)_{(i,j)}&=f_j,&\n (\\mathsf Jg)_{(i,j)}&=g_{(j,i)},\\\\\n (\\mathsf Ng)_{(i,j)}\n   &=\\frac1b\\sum_{\\substack{k\\sim j\\\\k\\ne i}}g_{(j,k)}.\n\\end{aligned}$$ Invariance makes the two lifts isometries and makes $\\mathsf J$ a self-adjoint isometry with $\\mathsf J^2=I$. Jensen’s inequality makes $\\mathsf N$ a contraction. The adjoints of the lifts are $$\\label{eq:lift-adjoints}\n (\\mathsf F^*g)_v=\\frac1d\\sum_{u\\sim v}g_{(v,u)},\\qquad\n (\\mathsf H^*g)_v=\\frac1d\\sum_{u\\sim v}g_{(u,v)}.$$ Indeed, in the edge inner product we may average the fixed outgoing edge over all neighbors of its tail, because the tail stabilizer is transitive on those neighbors. This proves the first formula. Interchanging the endpoints first proves the second. In particular, $$\\mathsf F^*\\mathsf H=\\mathsf H^*\\mathsf F=\\mathsf A_\\pi,\\qquad\n \\mathsf J\\mathsf F=\\mathsf H,\\qquad\n \\mathsf J\\mathsf H=\\mathsf F.$$ Direct substitution in the definitions gives $$\\begin{aligned}\n \\mathsf N&=\\frac db\\,\\mathsf H\\mathsf F^*-\\frac1b\\,\\mathsf J,\n &\\mathsf N^*&=\\frac db\\,\\mathsf F\\mathsf H^*-\\frac1b\\,\\mathsf J,\n \\label{eq:edge-operator}\\\\\n \\mathsf N\\mathsf F&=\\mathsf H,\n &\\mathsf N\\mathsf H&=\\frac db\\,\\mathsf H\\mathsf A_\\pi-\\frac1b\\,\\mathsf F.\n \\label{eq:edge-lifts}\n\\end{aligned}$$ Thus $(\\mathsf N^*g)_{(i,j)}\n=b^{-1}\\sum_{k\\sim i,\\ k\\ne j}g_{(k,i)}$ and $\\mathsf N^*=\\mathsf J\\mathsf N\\mathsf J$.",
          "source_id": "PM-236.1"
        }
      ],
      "exact_definition": "A vertex assignment can be read at either endpoint of a directed edge. We compare these two lifts and then isolate the part of an edge assignment orthogonal to both. On the joined assignment spaces define the tail and head lifts $\\mathsf F,\\mathsf H:\\mathcal H_{\\mathrm v}(\\pi)\\to\n\\mathcal H_{\\mathrm e}(\\pi)$, the reversal $\\mathsf J$, and the forward nonbacktracking average $\\mathsf N$ by $$\\begin{aligned}\n (\\mathsf Ff)_{(i,j)}&=f_i,&\n (\\mathsf Hf)_{(i,j)}&=f_j,&\n (\\mathsf Jg)_{(i,j)}&=g_{(j,i)},\\\\\n (\\mathsf Ng)_{(i,j)}\n   &=\\frac1b\\sum_{\\substack{k\\sim j\\\\k\\ne i}}g_{(j,k)}.\n\\end{aligned}$$ Invariance makes the two lifts isometries and makes $\\mathsf J$ a self-adjoint isometry with $\\mathsf J^2=I$. Jensen’s inequality makes $\\mathsf N$ a contraction. The adjoints of the lifts are $$\\label{eq:lift-adjoints}\n (\\mathsf F^*g)_v=\\frac1d\\sum_{u\\sim v}g_{(v,u)},\\qquad\n (\\mathsf H^*g)_v=\\frac1d\\sum_{u\\sim v}g_{(u,v)}.$$ Indeed, in the edge inner product we may average the fixed outgoing edge over all neighbors of its tail, because the tail stabilizer is transitive on those neighbors. This proves the first formula. Interchanging the endpoints first proves the second. In particular, $$\\mathsf F^*\\mathsf H=\\mathsf H^*\\mathsf F=\\mathsf A_\\pi,\\qquad\n \\mathsf J\\mathsf F=\\mathsf H,\\qquad\n \\mathsf J\\mathsf H=\\mathsf F.$$ Direct substitution in the definitions gives $$\\begin{aligned}\n \\mathsf N&=\\frac db\\,\\mathsf H\\mathsf F^*-\\frac1b\\,\\mathsf J,\n &\\mathsf N^*&=\\frac db\\,\\mathsf F\\mathsf H^*-\\frac1b\\,\\mathsf J,\n \\label{eq:edge-operator}\\\\\n \\mathsf N\\mathsf F&=\\mathsf H,\n &\\mathsf N\\mathsf H&=\\frac db\\,\\mathsf H\\mathsf A_\\pi-\\frac1b\\,\\mathsf F.\n \\label{eq:edge-lifts}\n\\end{aligned}$$ Thus $(\\mathsf N^*g)_{(i,j)}\n=b^{-1}\\sum_{k\\sim i,\\ k\\ne j}g_{(k,i)}$ and $\\mathsf N^*=\\mathsf J\\mathsf N\\mathsf J$.",
      "interpretation": "Tail and head lifts, edge reversal and forward nonbacktracking average on joined edge assignments.",
      "kind": "definition",
      "name": "nonbacktracking and lift operators",

          "anchor": "eq:osgood-size",
          "exact_quote": "$$K_T>\\max\\{1,H_T^2\\},\\qquad\n I(t)=1+\\log\\frac{K_T}{V(t)}\\quad\\text{when }V(t)>0.\n \\label{eq:osgood-size}$$ In particular $I(t)>1$, including at times when $V(t)\\ge1$.",

      "exact_definition": "$$K_T>\\max\\{1,H_T^2\\},\\qquad\n I(t)=1+\\log\\frac{K_T}{V(t)}\\quad\\text{when }V(t)>0.\n \\label{eq:osgood-size}$$ In particular $I(t)>1$, including at times when $V(t)\\ge1$.",
      "interpretation": "Logarithmic inverse-variance scale used in the critical comparison.",
      "kind": "definition",
      "name": "Osgood scale of the discrepancy",

      "definition_anchor": "eq:variance-cutoff",
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "eq:variance-cutoff",
          "exact_quote": "Choose $C_1$ sufficiently large that $$C_1\\ge C_0',\\qquad \\gamma C_1\\log b\\ge1,\n \\qquad (\\alpha/2)b^{\\gamma C_1}\\ge2,$$ and take the integer $$R=\\left\\lceil C_1\\bigl(\\omega^{-1}+\\log I\\bigr)\\right\\rceil.\n \\label{eq:variance-cutoff}$$ The weighted estimates apply for $r>R$. Since $\\omega^{-1}\\ge1$ and $I>1$, the choices above also give $$(\\alpha/2)b^{\\gamma r}\n \\ge (\\alpha/2)b^{\\gamma C_1\\omega^{-1}}\n                  I^{\\gamma C_1\\log b}\n \\ge 2I \\qquad(r>R).$$ Splitting the exceptional exponent into equal halves therefore gives $$e^{-\\alpha b^{\\gamma r}}\n \\le e^{-2I}e^{-(\\alpha/2)b^{\\gamma r}}\n =e^{-2}\\left(\\frac{V}{K_T}\\right)^2\n       e^{-(\\alpha/2)b^{\\gamma r}}\n \\le V^2 e^{-(\\alpha/2)b^{\\gamma r}}\\quad(r>R).\n \\label{eq:exception-absorption}$$ The last step uses $K_T>1$, and so remains valid when $V\\ge1$.",
          "source_id": "PM-236.1"
        }
      ],
      "exact_definition": "Choose $C_1$ sufficiently large that $$C_1\\ge C_0',\\qquad \\gamma C_1\\log b\\ge1,\n \\qquad (\\alpha/2)b^{\\gamma C_1}\\ge2,$$ and take the integer $$R=\\left\\lceil C_1\\bigl(\\omega^{-1}+\\log I\\bigr)\\right\\rceil.\n \\label{eq:variance-cutoff}$$ The weighted estimates apply for $r>R$. Since $\\omega^{-1}\\ge1$ and $I>1$, the choices above also give $$(\\alpha/2)b^{\\gamma r}\n \\ge (\\alpha/2)b^{\\gamma C_1\\omega^{-1}}\n                  I^{\\gamma C_1\\log b}\n \\ge 2I \\qquad(r>R).$$ Splitting the exceptional exponent into equal halves therefore gives $$e^{-\\alpha b^{\\gamma r}}\n \\le e^{-2I}e^{-(\\alpha/2)b^{\\gamma r}}\n =e^{-2}\\left(\\frac{V}{K_T}\\right)^2\n       e^{-(\\alpha/2)b^{\\gamma r}}\n \\le V^2 e^{-(\\alpha/2)b^{\\gamma r}}\\quad(r>R).\n \\label{eq:exception-absorption}$$ The last step uses $K_T>1$, and so remains valid when $V\\ge1$.",
      "interpretation": "Integer path length splitting short paths, handled by variance bounds, from long ones, handled by attenuation.",

| id | quantity | relation | value | regime |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| q-ising-threshold | hyperbolic tangent of the inverse temperature | ≤ | $(d-1)^{-1/2}$ | integer degree at least three, nonnegative inverse temperature |
| q-site-field-deviation | site field deviation from the raw observation | ≤ | $d\beta$ | every input and time |
| q-cavity-field-deviation | component field deviation from the raw observation | ≤ | $b\beta$ | every input and time |
| q-message-bound | branch message magnitude | ≤ | $\beta$ | all real arguments |
| q-information-constant | lower information constant | = | $1/5$ | critical parameter |
| q-critical-information-upper | component second moment | ≤ | $C\omega(t)$ | every positive time, constant depending only on the degree |
| q-path-attenuation | conditional expectation of squared path product | ≤ | $e^{-c_0\omega(t)r}$ | every positive time, path length at least one |
| q-row-energy-subcritical | squared response row | ≤ | $1+\frac{d\theta^2}{1-b\theta^2}$ | all times |
| q-row-energy-critical | expected squared response row | ≤ | $\frac{C}{\omega(t)}$ | positive times |
| q-spectral-radius | spectral radius of the neighbor average on centered assignments | = | $\frac{2\sqrt b}{d}$ | centered equivariant vertex assignments |
| q-discrepancy-bound | site field discrepancy between copies | ≤ | $2T+2d\beta$ | every vertex, times up to the horizon |
| q-path-variance-constant | variance of a path sum of edge differences | ≤ | $C_d rS$ | simple paths of every length at least one |
| q-cut-constant | cut cost constant | = | $\exp\!\left(\frac{4\beta}{1-\theta}\right)$ | independent of time and length |
| q-path-average-tail | probability that a path average is large | ≤ | $\exp\left(-\frac{b^{\ell/4}}{2b}\right)$ | lengths beyond the threshold |
| q-critical-moments | absolute moments of the component field | ≤ | $e^{Kp^2}t^{p/4}$ | integer orders, small times |
| q-critical-field-tail | probability the field exceeds a small power of time | ≤ | $\exp\bigl(-c\log^2(1/t)\bigr)$ | small positive times |

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **step-posterior** [claim](#stmt-lem-posterior) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-posterior): Finite cavity recursion by Bayes, path product by iterated affine conditional means, then exhaustion by balls and a limsup version. -> cavity identities, field bounds and path-product covariance
- **step-binary-fusion** [claim](#stmt-lem-binary-fusion) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-binary-fusion): Densities relative to the signal laws, symmetrization of the combined posterior. -> explicit second moment with nonnegative loss
- **step-critical-information** [claim](#stmt-lem-critical-information) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-critical-information): Weighted linear estimator for the lower bound; fusion loss with a cancelling linear term for the upper bound. -> two-sided small-time information scale
- **step-component-tilts** [claim](#stmt-lem-component-spin-tilts) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-component-spin-tilts): Condition indicators of the neighbor spin on the component history. -> bounded linear tilts
- **step-path-attenuation** [claim](#stmt-lem-path-attenuation) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-path-attenuation): Conditional variance of a side message, shifted tanh lower bound, then integrate one intact side component at a time. -> exponential decay of squared path products
- **step-row-energy** [claim](#stmt-prop-row-energy) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-row-energy): Shell counting with the pointwise bound below the threshold; Tonelli with path attenuation at equality. -> integrable response rows
- **step-continuous-augmentation** [claim](#stmt-lem-continuous-augmentation) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-continuous-augmentation): Reverse martingale convergence for decreasing completed sigma-fields. -> martingale property in the usual augmentation
- **step-finite-energy** [claim](#stmt-lem-finite-energy) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-finite-energy): Itô formula for a smooth function of the finite terminal vector, then Itô isometry. -> finite energy identity
- **step-gaussian-path-tail** [claim](#stmt-lem-gaussian-path-tail) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-gaussian-path-tail): Gaussian density bound gives a uniform loss per factor; integrate terminal noises along the path. -> geometric decay away from time zero
- **step-step-approximation** [claim](#stmt-lem-observation-step-approximation) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-observation-step-approximation): Finite-ball coefficients sampled on a mesh, diagonal sequence, predictable representative. -> predictable integrands
- **step-innovations** [claim](#stmt-prop-innovations) / [proof](#pm-s0): Lévy characterization, energy limit with tail control, testing identity eliminating a residual martingale, continuous versions, Itô formula for atanh with stopping. -> infinite field and mean equations
- **step-compatible-copies** [claim](#stmt-prop-compatible-copies) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-compatible-copies): Future increments independent of the observation past, monotone class, Lévy characterization, kernel equivariance, transport of integrals by elementary approximation. -> joined filtration equations
- **step-copy-variance** [claim](#stmt-lem-copy-variance) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-copy-variance): Subtract the field equations and apply Itô to the squared discrepancy. -> variance derivative formula
- **step-strict-uniqueness** [claim](#stmt-prop-strict-uniqueness) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-strict-uniqueness): Cauchy–Schwarz on edge sums with convergent shell series, then Gronwall. -> vanishing discrepancy below threshold
- **step-marginal-spectrum** [claim](#stmt-lem-marginal-spectrum) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-marginal-spectrum): Exponential correlation decay of local assignments, even moments via random walk, spectral measure support, density of local assignments. -> spectral radius bound
- **step-path-variance** [claim](#stmt-prop-path-variance) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-marginal-spectrum): Transfer the spectral bound to differences, bound nonbacktracking powers by a Chebyshev recurrence, sum lag correlations. -> linear path variance
- **step-cut-continuation** [claim](#stmt-lem-cut-continuation) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-cut-continuation): Delete the entering message, propagate the contracted error, then integrate side components from the far end. -> locality at bounded cost with attenuation
- **step-path-average** [claim](#stmt-prop-path-average) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-path-average): Split at a cut level, conditional independence of descendant components, Hoeffding inequality. -> concentration of path averages
- **step-weighted-paths** [claim](#stmt-prop-weighted-paths) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-weighted-paths): Split each path in halves, average continuations by stabilizer transitivity, use concentration against bounded dependent weights. -> weighted path bounds
- **step-critical-tail** [claim](#stmt-lem-critical-tail) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-critical-tail): Base moments from the information bound, induction on the order using the strict contraction for higher powers, Markov inequality. -> all moments and small-field tail
- **step-small-log-loss** [claim](#stmt-lem-small-log-loss) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-small-log-loss): Mean-value bound on the small-field event, tail bound on the complement, balancing by the size of the variance. -> improved log-factor comparison
- **step-cutoff-sums** [claim](#stmt-lem-cutoff-sums) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-cutoff-sums): Short paths by unweighted bounds, long paths by attenuation sums, exceptional terms absorbed. -> noise and drift path sums
- **step-critical-uniqueness** [claim](#stmt-prop-critical-uniqueness) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-critical-uniqueness): Insert path sums into the variance identity, derive an integrable Osgood-type inequality, contradiction at the left endpoint of a positivity interval. -> vanishing discrepancy at the critical parameter
- **step-pointwise-factor** [claim](#stmt-lem-pointwise-spin-factor) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-pointwise-spin-factor): Local conditional expectations averaged over finite ball automorphism groups, martingale convergence, limsup thresholding. -> everywhere equivariant spin factor
- **step-factor-extraction** [claim](#stmt-prop-factor-extraction) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-factor-extraction): Equal copies give a point-mass kernel, Borel recovery map by dyadic interpolation, spins from integer-time slopes, Borel Brownian sampler. -> spin factor and the positive implication of the main theorem
- **step-main-theorem** [claim](#stmt-thm-sharp) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-factor-extraction): Positive implication from the recovery proposition; converse from the known correlation obstruction. -> sharp threshold

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **inv-sharp-threshold**: For every degree at least three and every inverse temperature, the free zero-field Ising law on the regular tree is a factor of IID exactly when the stated bound on the hyperbolic tangent of the inverse temperature holds.
- **inv-everywhere-equivariance**: In the permitted range the spin factor can be chosen to commute with every tree automorphism on every label input, not only almost surely.
- **inv-zero-discrepancy-below-threshold**: Below the threshold the discrepancy variance between the two conditional copies vanishes at all times.
- **inv-zero-discrepancy-critical**: At the critical coupling the discrepancy variance of the invariant conditional coupling with common innovations also vanishes at all times.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **anti-supercritical-sampling**: Use the construction to generate the free Ising state from independent labels when the coupling exceeds the critical value. -> The correlation decay required of any factor of IID is violated, so no such factor exists there.
- **anti-finite-radius-rule**: Implement the factor as a local rule that reads only labels within a bounded radius of each vertex. -> The map is only measurable and need not depend on finitely many labels; no coding radius is available.
- **anti-non-tree-graphs**: Apply the cavity-field and path-product arguments to graphs with cycles or irregular degrees. -> The component factorisations used rely on the tree structure and regular degree.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `not_applicable`. The main result is an existence theorem for a measurable map on an infinite tree with uncountably many independent labels; the proof gives no finite procedure, and the source states that no finitary coding or coding-radius estimate is asserted.

<a id="pm-s5"></a>
## 5. Domain engineering contracts

| domain | applies | strength | maturity | basis | why |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| software | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper proves that a Borel factor exists. It gives no finite procedure, API or runnable specification that a program could implement. |
| games_simulation | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The factor lives on an infinite tree and needs infinitely many labels and Brownian paths. A finite simulation cannot realise it exactly. |
| tools | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No checker, solver or verifier emerges from the argument. The content is an existence proof for a measurable map. |
| algorithms | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The positive direction is existential. It rests on limits, conditional expectations and a standard Borel sampling theorem, and gives no step count or complexity. |
| data_structures | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper introduces no data structure. Its objects are measurable path spaces, kernels and operators on Hilbert spaces. |
| hardware_accelerators | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | There is no finite parallel dataflow to map onto hardware. The map needs infinitely many continuous Brownian paths on an infinite tree. |
| crypto_security | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Nothing is stated about pseudorandomness, hardness or adversaries. The result concerns the law of spins, not security. |
| machine_learning | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Posterior fields appear only as analytical objects for a specific planted Gaussian model. No learning task, loss or training procedure is given. |
| optimisation_operations_research | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No objective, constraint set or optimisation problem is solved. The Osgood-type comparison is a uniqueness argument, not a search method. |
| distributed_systems | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The factor is root-free, which is suggestive for leaderless protocols. But it is not finitary, so no bounded-round message-passing protocol follows. |
| other | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The natural home is probability and statistical mechanics: factors of IID, stochastic localization and the Ising threshold on trees. This is mathematical content, not an engineering construction. |

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **limit-threshold-range** (hypothesis): The positive construction is proved only for degree at least three and coupling at or below the critical value; above it the correlation bound rules out any factor of IID. Blocked use: ['Using the construction to generate the free Ising state from independent labels above the threshold']
- **limit-non-finitary** (nonconstructive): The spin factor is only measurable. No finitary coding or coding-radius estimate is asserted, so it cannot be read as a bounded-radius local rule. Blocked use: ['Implementing the factor as a bounded-round or bounded-radius distributed protocol', 'Quoting a coding radius or running time for it']
- **limit-no-uniform-bounds** (approximation): The map is obtained for each fixed degree and temperature in the permitted range. No parameter-uniform or computational bound is asserted. Blocked use: ['Treating constants as uniform in the degree or temperature', 'Using the result as an effective algorithmic guarantee']
- **limit-null-set-equivariance** (formal_scope): Equivariance in the defining convention holds almost surely for each fixed automorphism, with a null set that may depend on the automorphism. Everywhere equivariance is obtained afterwards by a separate averaging lemma. Blocked use: ['Assuming a single null set works for all automorphisms at once']
- **limit-critical-comparison-stage** (edge_case): At equality the geometric decay of the shell series disappears, so uniqueness of the conditional copies relies on the Osgood-type contradiction argument, which gives no explicit procedure. Blocked use: ['Extracting an explicit construction or rate from the critical uniqueness step']
- **limit-infinite-component-estimate** (hypothesis): The path attenuation estimate uses intact infinite side branches. It is not a claim about arbitrarily truncated branches, where a separate estimate is needed. Blocked use: ['Applying the attenuation bound to finite truncations of the tree without the extra estimate']

<a id="pm-formal"></a>
### Formal statement (Lean 4)

Corpus status: formalized in the source corpus (toolchain `leanprover/lean4:v4.34.1`, Mathlib `d13f23b723b8a846827a245b89c10fc7d3f11612`). Not re-checked here; the statements give the exact quantifiers and hypotheses, not a proof.

Scope note of family 236 (`lean/docs/236.md`, verbatim):

> The following describes the scope of the Lean formalization related to the following accompanying paper(s):
>
> - [The sharp factor-of-IID threshold for the free Ising model on regular trees](../../preprints/The-sharp-factor-of-IID-threshold-for-the-free-Ising-model-on-regular-trees-September-26-2026/article.pdf)
>
> ## Scope
>
> The formalized result determines the factor-of-IID threshold for the free zero-field Ising law on the infinite $d$-regular tree. For every $d\ge3$ and $\beta\ge0$, the law is a factor of IID exactly when $\tanh\beta\le1/\sqrt{d-1}$, including equality. The factor uses independent uniform vertex labels and is almost surely equivariant for each fixed tree automorphism. Finitary coding and coding-radius bounds are not included.

Comparator source `FreeIsing.lean` (3042 bytes, sha256 `cf2fd894b22f094ccc071e57557c916ec3dc3a5664a45b0f106ff4e7bbfebcca`), verbatim:

```lean
import Mathlib

namespace OAI

noncomputable section

open MeasureTheory

namespace Problem367

abbrev TreeVertex (d : ℕ) :=
  {word : List (Fin d) // List.IsChain (fun first second : Fin d => first ≠ second) word}

def treeAdj {d : ℕ} (u v : TreeVertex d) : Prop :=
  (∃ c : Fin d, v.1 = u.1 ++ [c]) ∨
  (∃ c : Fin d, u.1 = v.1 ++ [c])

structure TreeAut (d : ℕ) where
  toEquiv : TreeVertex d ≃ TreeVertex d
  map_adj : ∀ u v, treeAdj (toEquiv u) (toEquiv v) ↔ treeAdj u v

def relabel {d : ℕ} {α : Type*} (g : TreeAut d)
    (x : TreeVertex d → α) : TreeVertex d → α :=
  fun v => x (g.toEquiv.symm v)

inductive IsingSpin where
  | neg
  | pos
  deriving DecidableEq

protected abbrev IsingSpin.enumList : List IsingSpin := [.neg, .pos]

protected theorem IsingSpin.enumList_getElem?_ctorIdx_eq (x : IsingSpin) :
    IsingSpin.enumList[x.ctorIdx]? = some x := by
  cases x <;> rfl

protected theorem IsingSpin.enumList_nodup : IsingSpin.enumList.Nodup := by decide

instance : Fintype IsingSpin where
  elems := ⟨IsingSpin.enumList, IsingSpin.enumList_nodup⟩
  complete x := by cases x <;> decide

instance isingSpinMeasurable : MeasurableSpace IsingSpin := ⊤

def uniform01 : Measure ℝ :=
  volume.restrict (Set.Icc (0 : ℝ) 1)

instance uniform01Probability : IsProbabilityMeasure uniform01 where
  measure_univ := by simp [uniform01]

def labelMeasure (d : ℕ) : Measure (TreeVertex d → ℝ) := by
  classical
  exact Measure.infinitePi (fun _ : TreeVertex d => uniform01)

def ConnectedFinset {d : ℕ} (D : Finset (TreeVertex d)) : Prop :=
  D.Nonempty ∧
    ∀ u ∈ D, ∀ v ∈ D,
      Relation.ReflTransGen
        (fun x y : TreeVertex d => x ∈ D ∧ y ∈ D ∧ treeAdj x y) u v

def inducedEdges {d : ℕ} (D : Finset (TreeVertex d)) :
    Finset (TreeVertex d × TreeVertex d) := by
  classical
  exact (D.product D).filter (fun e =>
    treeAdj e.1 e.2 ∧ e.1.1.length < e.2.1.length)

def freeCylinderWeight {d : ℕ} (theta : ℝ)
    (D : Finset (TreeVertex d)) (eta : TreeVertex d → IsingSpin) : ENNReal :=
  ENNReal.ofReal
    ((1 / 2 : ℝ) *
      Finset.prod (inducedEdges D) (fun e =>
        (1 + (if eta e.1 = eta e.2 then theta else -theta)) / 2))

def HasFreeIsingLaw {d : ℕ} (theta : ℝ)
    (mu : Measure (TreeVertex d → IsingSpin)) : Prop :=
  ∀ (D : Finset (TreeVertex d)), ConnectedFinset D →
    ∀ eta : TreeVertex d → IsingSpin,
      mu {x | ∀ v ∈ D, x v = eta v} = freeCylinderWeight theta D eta

def IsFactorOfIID (d : ℕ) (theta : ℝ) : Prop :=
  ∃ Phi : (TreeVertex d → ℝ) → (TreeVertex d → IsingSpin),
    Measurable Phi ∧
    HasFreeIsingLaw theta (Measure.map Phi (labelMeasure d)) ∧
    ∀ g : TreeAut d,
      ∀ᵐ U ∂labelMeasure d, Phi (relabel g U) = relabel g (Phi U)

theorem free_ising_factor_iff_threshold :
    ∀ (d : ℕ) (beta : ℝ), 3 ≤ d → 0 ≤ beta → (IsFactorOfIID d (Real.tanh beta) ↔ Real.tanh beta ≤ 1 / Real.sqrt ((d : ℝ) - 1)) := by
  sorry

end Problem367

end

end OAI
```

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


For every integer <i>d</i> ≥ 3 and inverse temperature <i>β</i> ≥ 0, the free zero-field ferromagnetic Ising measure on the infinite <i>d</i>-regular tree is a factor of IID exactly when $`\tanh\beta\le(d-1)^{-1/2}`$. We prove the positive implication, including equality, resolving the conjecture of Nam, Sly and Zhang; the strict converse is known. The spin factor can be chosen to commute with every tree automorphism on every label input.

<a id="sec:introduction"></a>
### Introduction

Can the free Ising state on a regular tree be generated from independent vertex labels without choosing a root? The broadcast description of the state begins at a vertex and propagates a random spin along the edges. Its law does not depend on that vertex. A factor construction must also remove the distinguished vertex from the rule that generates the spins.

Let $T_d=(\mathcal V,\mathcal E)$ be the infinite $d$-regular tree, $d\ge3$, and put $b=d-1$ and $\theta=\tanh\beta$ for $\beta\ge0$. The free zero-field Ising law $\mu_{d,\beta}$ is obtained by choosing a fair spin at one vertex and, independently along edges directed away from it, letting a child agree with its parent with probability $(1+\theta)/2$. Its marginal on any finite connected subtree $D$ is $$\mu_{d,\beta}(\sigma|_D=\eta)
   =\frac12\prod_{\{u,v\}\in\mathcal E(D)}
                      \frac{1+\theta\eta_u\eta_v}{2}.$$ This expression also verifies that the law is invariant under all tree automorphisms and independent of the chosen broadcast root.

<a id="eq:equivariance"></a>
We call a spin law a *factor of IID* if it is the law of $\Phi(U)$ for independent uniform labels $(U_v)_{v\in\mathcal V}$ and a measurable map $\Phi:[0,1]^{\mathcal V}\to\{-1,1\}^{\mathcal V}$ satisfying $$\label{eq:equivariance}
 \Phi(gU)=g\Phi(U)\quad\text{almost surely for each fixed }
                 g\in\mathop{\mathrm{Aut}}(T_d),\qquad (gx)_v=x_{g^{-1}v}.$$ The exceptional null set may depend on $g$. For spin laws this convention is equivalent to requiring a Borel factor equivariant on every input: Lemma <a href="#lem:pointwise-spin-factor" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:pointwise-spin-factor">25</a> constructs such a version. The input contains no distinguished root or end. Measurability does not require a spin to be determined by finitely many labels; no finitary coding or coding-radius estimate is asserted.

<a id="stmt-thm-sharp"></a>
<div id="thm:sharp" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1**. *<span id="thm:main" label="thm:main"></span>For every integer $d\ge3$ and every $\beta\ge0$, the free zero-field Ising law $\mu_{d,\beta}$ is a factor of IID in the sense of <a href="#eq:equivariance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:equivariance">[eq:equivariance]</a> if and only if $$\tanh\beta\le(d-1)^{-1/2}.$$ In this range the factor can be chosen to commute with every automorphism on every label input.*

</div>

The construction proves the positive implication, including equality. The strict converse is known: the obstruction beyond the reconstruction threshold is due to Sly, as recorded and proved in Lyons (Lyons 2017, sec. 3 and Corollary 3.2) <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[Lyons](#ref-Lyons)]. We use the quantitative correlation bound of Backhausz, Szegedy and Virág (Backhausz, Szegedy, and Virág 2015, Theorem 3.1 in arXiv:1305.6784v1) <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[BSV](#ref-BSV)] to give that implication below. The map is obtained for each fixed $d$ and $\beta$ in the permitted range; no parameter-uniform or computational bound is asserted.

For the free state on $T_d$, reconstruction from distant levels is possible exactly when $b\theta^2>1$; equivalently, the free Gibbs state is extremal exactly when $b\theta^2\le1$. For the regular-tree result, including equality, see the Bethe-lattice work of Bleher, Ruiz, and Zagrebnov (Bleher, Ruiz, and Zagrebnov 1995) <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[BleherRuizZagrebnov1995](#ref-BleherRuizZagrebnov1995)] and Ioffe (Ioffe 1996b) <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[Ioffe1996Bethe](#ref-Ioffe1996Bethe)]. Ioffe (Ioffe 1996a) and Evans, Kenyon, Peres, and Schulman (Evans et al. 2000) <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[EvansKenyonPeresSchulman2000](#ref-EvansKenyonPeresSchulman2000)] developed branching-number criteria on general trees away from equality. Pemantle and Peres (Pemantle and Peres 2010, Theorem 2.1) <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[PemantlePeres](#ref-PemantlePeres)] later gave an electrical-capacity criterion for free-state extremality for couplings bounded above and away from zero, which also covers critical cases.

Reconstruction asks whether distant spins retain information about a fixed spin. The factor question asks for a rule that generates the entire law from independent labels. Nonreconstruction supplies no such rule, and a weak limit of factor laws need not itself be a factor (Lyons 2017, Corollary 3.3) <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[Lyons](#ref-Lyons)]. Thus equality requires a construction that retains measurable dependence on its independent input.

In the uniqueness range $\theta\le b^{-1}$, including equality, a finite-cluster construction gives the factor; see Lyons (Lyons 2017, sec. 3) <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[Lyons](#ref-Lyons)]. Nam, Sly and Zhang (Nam, Sly, and Zhang 2022, Theorem 1 and Section 2.2 in arXiv:2012.09484v2) <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[NSZ](#ref-NSZ)] used posterior-drift stochastic differential equations driven by IID Brownian motions to construct the free Ising law as a factor for $d\ge d_0$ and $\theta\le c/\sqrt{d-1}$, for unspecified absolute constants $c,d_0>0$. Their construction takes root-independent limits of finite-volume solutions. Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">[thm:main]</a> proves their conjecture for every $d\ge3$, including equality and their pointwise convention for equivariance.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
###### The known converse.

For a centered variance-one real vertex factor, the BSV bound is $$|\mathbb E[Z_oZ_v]|\le
       \left(1+\frac{d-2}{d}n\right)b^{-n/2},
                  \qquad \mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(o,v)=n.$$ It applies also under <a href="#eq:equivariance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:equivariance">[eq:equivariance]</a>. To check this, condition the observable at $o$ on the labels in its radius-$R$ ball and average a Borel version over the finite automorphism group of that rooted ball. Every such permutation extends to a tree automorphism fixing $o$, so uniqueness of conditional expectation leaves this average unchanged almost surely. It now defines an exactly invariant local rule. Apply the same rule at every vertex. These local factors converge in $L^2$ at each vertex to the original factor, because the balls exhaust the label coordinates. For all sufficiently large $R$ their variances are positive. Center and normalize them, apply BSV, and pass to the limit in variances and covariances on this same IID probability space. Since the Ising spins have mean zero, variance one and correlation $\theta^n$, the bound would require $(\theta\sqrt b)^n\le1+(d-2)n/d$. This is impossible when $\theta>b^{-1/2}$.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### Why independent innovations determine the spins

We follow the observation framework of Nam–Sly–Zhang. On an auxiliary probability space, sample the desired spin configuration $\sigma$ and independent standard Brownian motions $B_v$, and observe $$X_v(t)=t\sigma_v+B_v(t),\qquad
 m_v(t)=\mathbb E[\sigma_v\mid X_u(s):u\in\mathcal V,\ 0\le s\le t].$$ The finite-dimensional posterior evolution also belongs to stochastic localization. Eldan (Eldan 2013) <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[Eldan2013](#ref-Eldan2013)] developed the underlying localization scheme, and El Alaoui and Montanari (El Alaoui and Montanari 2022, sec. 3, Theorem 2) <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[ElAlaouiMontanari2022](#ref-ElAlaouiMontanari2022)] give the fixed-covariance Gaussian-observation formulation used here. Subtracting the conditional drift gives $$W_v(t)=X_v(t)-\int_0^t m_v(s)\,ds.$$ These innovations are independent Brownian motions in the observation filtration. This property alone does not make $X$ a function of $W$: the original construction still used the spins. Classical innovations theory separates the Brownian property from generation of the observation filtration (Fujisaki, Kallianpur, and Kunita 1972, Lemma 2.2 and Remark 2.2) <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[FujisakiKallianpurKunita1972](#ref-FujisakiKallianpurKunita1972)]. Proving that the innovations determine $X$ is the central step here.

A companion construction for the free uniform spanning forest uses a uniform Lipschitz response bound in the coordinate supremum norm to invert the innovations causally for every continuous input (OpenAI 2026, sec. 4) <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[OpenAIFUSF2026](#ref-OpenAIFUSF2026)]. Here recovery instead follows from equality of conditional copies under the observation law.

Take two observations $X,X'$ independently conditional on the entire innovation array $W$. They have the same marginal law and share the same innovations. Conditioning on the full paths could in principle expose their future increments through an observation past. We prove that it does not: the conditional law of either observation past given all of $W$ depends only on the past of $W$. Hence $W$ remains Brownian in the joined filtration, and the equations for both observations use the same driving Brownian motions. This is the compatibility principle for conditional copies (Kurtz 2014, Lemmas 2.11–2.12 in arXiv:1305.6747v2) <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[Kurtz2014](#ref-Kurtz2014)]; we prove the required statement and the transport of the stochastic integrals directly.

We then show that these particular copies agree. For each coordinate, conditional independence gives $$\mathbb E[(X_v(t)-X'_v(t))^2\mid W]
                  =2\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}(X_v(t)\mid W).$$ Equality therefore forces the conditional law of $X$ given $W$ to be a point mass. Finally $$\frac{X_v(n)}n\longrightarrow\sigma_v
                           \quad\text{almost surely}$$ recovers the spins from $W$. A common measurable sampler converts one uniform label at each vertex into its Brownian path. This gives a spin factor satisfying <a href="#eq:equivariance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:equivariance">[eq:equivariance]</a>; finite-ball averaging then selects an everywhere equivariant version with the same law.

<a id="pm-h-5"></a>
#### What changes at the critical point

The tree gives a concrete equation for comparing the copies. Set $h_v=\operatorname{atanh}m_v$. If $T_{j\to i}$ is the component containing $j$ after deleting $ij$, define its field by $$h_{j\to i}(t)=\operatorname{atanh}
   \mathbb E[\sigma_j\mid X_u(s):u\in T_{j\to i},\ 0\le s\le t],$$ using the unconditional prior on that component. For a path $v=v_0,\ldots,v_r=u$, the field-response coefficient is $$c_{vu}=\theta^r\prod_{i=1}^r a(h_{v_i\to v_{i-1}}),
 \qquad
 a(z)=\frac{1-\tanh^2z}{1-\theta^2\tanh^2z},\qquad c_{vv}=1.$$ The innovation equation is $$dh_v=\sum_u c_{vu}\,dW_u+m_v A_v\,dt,
                  \qquad A_v=\sum_u c_{vu}^2.$$ Its infinite sum requires proof. At criticality we obtain $\mathbb EA_v(t)\le C/\min(\sqrt t,1)$ for $t>0$, so the row is integrable down to time zero. An exact covariance-energy identity and a testing argument establish the equation without a residual martingale.

For the conditional copies, let $V(t)=\mathbb E[(h_o(t)-h'_o(t))^2]$; invariance makes the choice of $o$ irrelevant. It starts at zero. Below the threshold, the squared factor $\theta^{2r}$ and the shell size $d b^{r-1}$ give a summable geometric series, yielding $V'\le CV$ and thus equality of the copies. At $b\theta^2=1$ those two factors cancel exactly.

Three estimates replace the lost geometric decay. First, an intact side branch attenuates a path product on the length scale $t^{-1/2}$ at small times. Second, spatial cancellation bounds the variance of a sum of differences between the two copies’ $\log a(h_{j\to i})$ factors by the path length times the variance at one edge. The spectral bound is proved for differences $f(X)-f(X')$, with the same square-integrable equivariant vertex observable $f$ applied to each copy, and their closed linear span. No spectral bound for every function of the pair is assumed. The operator argument is related to the correlation methods of Backhausz–Szegedy–Virág (Backhausz, Szegedy, and Virág 2015) <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[BSV](#ref-BSV)] and Backhausz–Gerencsér–Harangi–Vizer (Backhausz et al. 2018) <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[BGHV](#ref-BGHV)]. Third, concentration of path averages makes the attenuation usable with discrepancy weights that depend on the same observations. Both path orientations are needed in this averaging argument.

These estimates leave one small-time difficulty: the leading noise term is of order $S(t)/t$, where $$S(t)=\mathbb E\Bigl[\bigl(\log a(h_{j\to i}(t))
                    -\log a(h'_{j\to i}(t))\bigr)^2\Bigr].$$ The elementary bound $S\le CV$ would leave a nonintegrable coefficient. We prove instead a small-field tail estimate and use the vanishing derivative of $\log a$ at zero to improve that comparison. The complete noise and drift calculation then gives an integrable Osgood-type inequality for $V$, forcing $V=0$. The moment estimate is proved beside this final comparison, where its purpose becomes explicit.

<a id="pm-h-6"></a>
###### Notation and organization.

The case $\theta=0$ is already IID, so the proof below assumes $0<\theta\le b^{-1/2}$. Constants $c,C>0$ may depend on the fixed $d,\theta$ and may change between occurrences; a subscript $T$ permits additional dependence on a fixed finite time horizon. Every path is simple unless specified otherwise, and all logarithms are natural. Roots used to describe subtrees or compute norms are auxiliary choices in the proof, not inputs to the factor.

Sections <a href="#sec:observations" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:observations">2</a>–<a href="#sec:innovations" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:innovations">4</a> develop the posterior identities, critical information bounds, and infinite innovation equation. Section <a href="#sec:coupling" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:coupling">5</a> constructs the conditional copies and proves their equality below the threshold. Sections <a href="#sec:spectrum" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:spectrum">6</a> and <a href="#sec:path-averages" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:path-averages">7</a> establish spatial cancellation and concentration of path averages. Section <a href="#sec:uniqueness-factor" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:uniqueness-factor">8</a> proves the small-field tail estimate, completes the critical comparison, and recovers the factor from the innovations.

<a id="sec:observations"></a>
### Posterior fields along the tree

The observation construction reduces the factor problem to a question about conditional spin laws. We first describe those laws on a finite subtree, where the Gaussian likelihood is explicit. The tree structure then expresses each covariance as a product along the connecting path. Passing these formulas to the whole tree will identify the response coefficients whose squared rows must be integrable in time.

For the affirmative implication, write $b=d-1$ and assume $0<\theta=\tanh\beta\le b^{-1/2}$; the case $\theta=0$ is already IID. On an auxiliary probability space sample the free Ising configuration $\sigma$, and independently sample standard Brownian motions $(B_v)_{v\in\mathcal V}$, independent across vertices. Set <a id="eq:observations"></a>$$\label{eq:observations}
 X_v(t)=t\sigma_v+B_v(t),\qquad
 \mathcal F_t^0
   =\sigma\{X_v(s):v\in\mathcal V,\ 0\le s\le t\}.$$ The $B_v$’s are the noise used to plant these observations. The innovations $W_v$, obtained later by subtracting the conditional drift from $X_v$, are different processes. In this section $\mathcal F_t^0$ is the raw observation filtration; we reserve $\mathcal F_t$ for its usual augmentation. For any $D\subseteq\mathcal V$, write $$\mathcal F_t^D
   =\sigma\{X_u(s):u\in D,\ 0\le s\le t\}.$$

The Gaussian observation framework follows Nam–Sly–Zhang (Nam, Sly, and Zhang 2022) <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[NSZ](#ref-NSZ)]. In finite volume it is the observation form of stochastic localization described by El Alaoui–Montanari (El Alaoui and Montanari 2022, sec. 3) <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[ElAlaouiMontanari2022](#ref-ElAlaouiMontanari2022)]. We derive the finite posterior directly, since its derivative and its tree factorization will both be needed.

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
#### The finite posterior and its branch messages

<a id="eq:finite-posterior"></a>
Let $D$ be a finite connected subtree. Its broadcast marginal is $$\mathbb P(\sigma_D=\eta)
   =2^{-|D|}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in\mathcal E(D)}
                (1+\theta\eta_i\eta_j)
   \ \propto\
     \exp\left(\beta\sum_{\{i,j\}\in\mathcal E(D)}
                              \eta_i\eta_j\right),
 \qquad \eta\in\{-1,1\}^D.$$ Indeed, choose any vertex of $D$ as the broadcast root and multiply its probability $1/2$ by the transition probabilities on the $|D|-1$ edges; also $1+\theta s=e^{\beta s}/\cosh\beta$ for $s\in\{-1,1\}$. Conditional on $\sigma_D=\eta$, the likelihood of the observation paths through time $t$, relative to product Wiener measure, is $$\exp\left(\sum_{u\in D}\eta_uX_u(t)-\frac{t|D|}{2}\right).$$ The last term is independent of $\eta$. Bayes’ formula gives, for every finite terminal vector $x\in\mathbb R^D$, $$\label{eq:finite-posterior}
 \pi_x^D(\eta)
  =\frac{1}{Z_D(x)}
     \exp\left(
       \beta\sum_{\{i,j\}\in\mathcal E(D)}\eta_i\eta_j
       +\sum_{u\in D}x_u\eta_u\right),
 \qquad x=X_D(t).$$ Here $Z_D(x)$ is the positive finite partition function. This calculation shows that the terminal vector is sufficient for the spin posterior; it does not identify the terminal-vector sigma-field with the entire observation history.

<a id="eq:finite-posterior-derivative"></a>
Let $$M_v^D(x)=\sum_\eta\eta_v\pi_x^D(\eta),\qquad
 m_v^D(t)=M_v^D(X_D(t)),\qquad
 h_v^D(t)=\operatorname{atanh}m_v^D(t).$$ By Bayes’ formula, $m_v^D(t)$ is a version of $\mathbb E[\sigma_v\mid\mathcal F_t^D]$. All posterior masses are positive, so $|M_v^D(x)|<1$ and the field is finite. The functions $M_v^D$ are smooth. If $u,v\in D$, then differentiating the finite sum in <a href="#eq:finite-posterior" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:finite-posterior">[eq:finite-posterior]</a> gives $$\label{eq:finite-posterior-derivative}
 \frac{\partial M_v^D}{\partial x_u}(x)
   =\mathop{\mathrm{Cov}}_{\pi_x^D}(\eta_v,\eta_u).$$ At $x=X_D(t)$ we denote this conditional covariance by $C_{vu}^D(t)$.

For an oriented edge $v\to w$, let $T_{v\to w}$ be the component containing $v$ after deletion of $\{v,w\}$. If both vertices belong to $D$, define $$h_{v\to w}^D(t)=h_v^{D\cap T_{v\to w}}(t).$$ Thus a component field uses only its own component’s observations and the unconditional free prior on that component. It is not the field at $v$ after observing the opposite side or specifying the spin at $w$. The same distinction will be essential on the infinite tree.

<a id="stmt-lem-posterior"></a>
<div id="lem:posterior" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:site-field-bound"></a>
<a id="eq:covariance"></a>
**Lemma 2** (Posterior identities). *For a fixed deterministic time $t\ge0$, set $$m_v(t)=\mathbb E[\sigma_v\mid\mathcal F_t^0],\qquad
 m_{v\to w}(t)
   =\mathbb E[\sigma_v\mid\mathcal F_t^{T_{v\to w}}].$$ These means lie in $(-1,1)$ almost surely. Write $h_v=\operatorname{atanh}m_v$ and $h_{v\to w}=\operatorname{atanh}m_{v\to w}$, and define $$\psi(z)=\operatorname{atanh}(\theta\tanh z),\qquad
 a(z)=\frac{1-\tanh^2z}{1-\theta^2\tanh^2z}.$$ At each fixed time, almost surely, <a id="eq:cavity"></a>$$\label{eq:cavity}
 \begin{aligned}
 h_{v\to w}(t)
   &=X_v(t)+\sum_{\substack{u\sim v\\u\ne w}}
                       \psi(h_{u\to v}(t)),\\
 h_v(t)
   &=X_v(t)+\sum_{u\sim v}\psi(h_{u\to v}(t))
     =h_{v\to w}(t)+\psi(h_{w\to v}(t)).
 \end{aligned}$$ The fields have causal, progressively measurable Borel versions as functions of the observation paths. The component version uses only paths in $T_{v\to w}$. These versions require no distinguished root and satisfy, at every input and time, $$\label{eq:site-field-bound}
 |h_v(t)-X_v(t)|\le d\beta,\qquad
 |h_{v\to w}(t)-X_v(t)|\le b\beta.$$ In the process formulas below, use these chosen field versions and set $m_v(t)=\tanh h_v(t)$ and $m_{v\to w}(t)=\tanh h_{v\to w}(t)$. For the simple path $v=v_0,v_1,\ldots,v_r=u$, define $$\label{eq:covariance}
 \begin{aligned}
 c_{vu}(t)
   &=\theta^r\prod_{i=1}^r
                  a(h_{v_i\to v_{i-1}}(t)),&
 c_{vv}(t)&=1,\\
 C_{vu}(t)&=(1-m_v(t)^2)c_{vu}(t).
 \end{aligned}$$ The last expression is a progressively measurable version of $\mathop{\mathrm{Cov}}(\sigma_v,\sigma_u\mid\mathcal F_t^0)$ at each fixed time. The cavity and covariance formulas also hold in every finite connected subtree, with branches restricted to that subtree. For all real $z$, $$0<a(z)\le1,\qquad |\psi(z)|\le\beta,\qquad
 \psi'(z)=\theta a(z)\in(0,\theta],$$ and $0\le C_{vu}(t)\le\theta^{\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(v,u)}$ at each fixed time.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-posterior"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-posterior</summary>

*Proof.* We first establish the identities in a finite connected subtree $D$. A branch whose root has field $z$ assigns that root probabilities proportional to $e^{z}$ and $e^{-z}$. Summing its root spin against the edge interaction gives a factor proportional to $\cosh(z+\beta s)$ when its parent spin is $s$. Its contribution to the parent’s half log-odds is therefore $$\frac12\log\frac{\cosh(z+\beta)}{\cosh(z-\beta)}
   =\operatorname{atanh}(\theta\tanh z)=\psi(z).$$ The branch likelihoods multiply because the components are disjoint conditional on the parent spin. Adding their half log-odds and the own observation $X_v(t)$ proves the finite cavity formulas. Since $|\psi|\le\beta$, the site field differs from $X_v(t)$ by at most $d\beta$, and a component field by at most $b\beta$.

We next follow a path $v_0,\ldots,v_r$ in $D$. With the observations fixed, sum the posterior in <a href="#eq:finite-posterior" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:finite-posterior">[eq:finite-posterior]</a> over all spins off the path. Given the preceding path spins, the conditional mean of the spin at $v_i$ uses only $\sigma_{v_{i-1}}$ and the field on the side excluding that predecessor: $$\mathbb E[\sigma_{v_i}\mid \sigma_{v_0},\ldots,\sigma_{v_{i-1}},
                    \mathcal F_t^D]
   =\tanh(z+\beta\sigma_{v_{i-1}})
   =p_i+q_i\sigma_{v_{i-1}},
 \qquad z=h_{v_i\to v_{i-1}}^D(t).$$ Here $p_i$ is an intercept depending on the observations, and $$q_i=\frac{\tanh(z+\beta)-\tanh(z-\beta)}{2}
     =\theta\,\frac{1-\tanh^2z}{1-\theta^2\tanh^2z}
     =\theta a(z).$$ Iterating these affine conditional means makes the slope of the conditional mean at $v_r$, as a function of $\sigma_{v_0}$, equal to $\prod_{i=1}^r q_i$. Taking covariance with the first spin gives $$C_{v_0v_r}^D(t)
   =(1-(m_{v_0}^D(t))^2)\prod_{i=1}^r q_i.$$ This proves the finite path formula, including the empty product at $r=0$. The coefficient $c_{vu}^D$ is directed even though the covariance is symmetric: its normalization uses the variance at $v$. In fact, <a href="#eq:finite-posterior-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:finite-posterior-derivative">[eq:finite-posterior-derivative]</a> gives $$\frac{\partial}{\partial x_u}\operatorname{atanh}M_v^D(x)
   =\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{Cov}}_{\pi_x^D}(\eta_v,\eta_u)}
           {1-M_v^D(x)^2},$$ which is the path product just obtained. Thus $c_{vu}^D$ is the finite field’s response to the observation at $u$.

Now fix $t$ and exhaust the tree by increasing finite balls. The conditional expectations of a fixed spin, or a product of two fixed spins, converge almost surely and in $L^2$ to conditioning on $\mathcal F_t^0$. This is increasing-sigma-field convergence for bounded random variables. Intersecting the balls with $T_{v\to w}$ gives the corresponding convergence for the component posterior. The finite field bounds place each mean in the random compact interval $$[\tanh(X_v(t)-k\beta),\,\tanh(X_v(t)+k\beta)]
       \ \subset\ (-1,1),\qquad k=d\ \hbox{or}\ b.$$ Thus the limiting means remain strictly inside $(-1,1)$, and their inverse hyperbolic tangents converge to finite fields. For a fixed path, take balls large enough to contain it. Their intersections with the finitely many directed components on the path exhaust those components. The finite cavity sums and path product therefore pass to the limit; convergence of the two-spin moments identifies the limit as the conditional covariance. Any other centered exhaustion gives the same conditional expectations, since its histories generate the same sigma-fields.

It remains to choose process versions without presupposing a continuous infinite-dimensional filter. On the canonical space of continuous observation paths, evaluate the finite field formulas on $B_R(v)$ for a site and on $B_R(v)\cap T_{v\to w}$ for a component, with $R=0,1,\ldots$. Define each raw field to be the pointwise $\limsup_{R\to\infty}$ of these finite fields. Each finite expression is a continuous adapted terminal-array function. Its upper limit is Borel and progressively measurable, for the whole observation filtration or for the specified component filtration respectively. The finite bounds make the upper limits finite and preserve <a href="#eq:site-field-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:site-field-bound">[eq:site-field-bound]</a> at every input and time. At each fixed time they agree almost surely with the conditional fields proved above. Their hyperbolic tangents and the finite products in <a href="#eq:covariance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:covariance">[eq:covariance]</a> give the claimed mean and covariance versions.

These formulas use only the terminal array at the evaluated time, and in particular only the histories up to that time. Centering every ball at its indexed vertex makes the formulas commute with graph relabeling. Joint measurability and Fubini’s theorem turn the fixed-time algebraic identities into identities for almost every time, almost surely. Countability of the vertices, oriented edges, finite paths, and integer time horizons permits all these time-probability statements to hold simultaneously. Continuity and identities simultaneous at every time are not needed here; they will be obtained after the filtering argument in Section <a href="#sec:innovations" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:innovations">4</a>.

Finally, direct differentiation gives $\psi'(z)=\theta(1-\tanh^2z)/(1-\theta^2\tanh^2z)$. Since $0<\theta<1$, its bounds and $0<a\le1$ follow. The covariance bound follows from the finite product and $1-m_v^2\le1$. ◻

</details>

The normalized response $c_{vu}$, rather than the covariance alone, is the coefficient that will occur in the field equation. At distance $r$, its deterministic bound is $\theta^r$. Below the threshold this already gives a summable squared row. At equality, the size of a distance shell cancels $\theta^{2r}$; the next section obtains the additional decay from the observations in side branches.

<a id="sec:critical-information"></a>
### Critical information controls the response row

At the critical parameter, the squared edge factor in <a href="#eq:covariance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:covariance">[eq:covariance]</a> is exactly balanced by the number of vertices at that distance. To sum a response row, we must therefore obtain decay from the factors $a(h_{v_i\to v_{i-1}})$. We will show that the observations in one intact infinite side branch supply a loss of order $\sqrt t$ at each path step for small $t$. The resulting expected squared row is of order at most $t^{-1/2}$, which is integrable at the initial time.

Until the final row estimate, assume $b=d-1\ge2$ and $\theta=b^{-1/2}$. Constants in the critical estimates depend only on the fixed degree $d$. We work at a fixed deterministic time $t>0$ with the raw posterior versions from Lemma <a href="#lem:posterior" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:posterior">2</a>; their continuity is not needed. For an oriented edge $v\to w$, put $$Y=h_{v\to w}(t),\qquad
 M(t)=\mathbb E\tanh^2Y,\qquad
 \omega(t)=\min\{\sqrt t,1\}.$$ The law of $Y$ is independent of the oriented edge. It is symmetric: reversing all spins and all observations preserves their joint law and negates the conditional field almost surely at this fixed time. We first determine the scale of $M(t)$. Its lower bound gives path decay, while the whole-site upper bound will control the early-time energy in the filtering limit.

<a id="pm-h-10"></a>
#### Combining symmetric binary signals

Combining two branches loses a definite amount of second-moment information compared with adding their separate contributions. This quadratic loss is related to Ioffe’s recursive second-moment estimates for Ising trees (Ioffe 1996a, Proposition 4) <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[Ioffe1996GeneralTrees](#ref-Ioffe1996GeneralTrees)]. The following elementary formulation will allow us to keep the loss explicit.

<a id="stmt-lem-binary-fusion"></a>
<div id="lem:binary-fusion" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:binary-fusion"></a>
**Lemma 3** (Symmetric binary-signal fusion). *Let $\varepsilon$ be a uniform sign and let two random elements of standard Borel spaces be independent conditional on $\varepsilon$. Let $U,V$ be the posterior means of $\varepsilon$ from the respective signals. Suppose that their marginal laws are symmetric and $|U|,|V|<1$ almost surely. If $\mathbb E_{\mathrm{prod}}$ denotes expectation under the product of these two marginal laws, then the posterior mean from both signals is $(U+V)/(1+UV)$, and its second moment is $$\label{eq:binary-fusion}
 \mathbb EU^2+\mathbb EV^2-
 \mathbb E_{\mathrm{prod}}
   \frac{U^2V^2(2-U^2-V^2)}{1-U^2V^2}.$$ In particular this second moment is at most $\mathbb EU^2+\mathbb EV^2$. If $|U|,|V|\le\theta$, the subtracted term is at least $2(1-\theta^2)\mathbb EU^2\,\mathbb EV^2$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-binary-fusion"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-binary-fusion</summary>

*Proof.* For a signal with marginal law $\nu$ and posterior mean $u$, the conditional signal law given $\varepsilon=\eta$ has density $1+\eta u$ with respect to $\nu$, for $\eta\in\{-1,1\}$. Indeed, condition the indicator of $\{\varepsilon=\eta\}$ on the signal and use its posterior probability $(1+\eta u)/2$. Conditional independence then gives joint marginal density $1+UV$ relative to the product of the two signal laws. The signed difference of the two conditional densities gives the combined posterior mean $(U+V)/(1+UV)$. Pushing the product signal law forward to the product of the marginal laws of $U,V$, its second moment is $$\mathbb E_{\mathrm{prod}}\frac{(U+V)^2}{1+UV}.$$ The integrand is at most $2$, so it is legitimate to average it under the symmetry $V\mapsto -V$. The result is $$\frac{U^2+V^2-2U^2V^2}{1-U^2V^2}
   =U^2+V^2-
       \frac{U^2V^2(2-U^2-V^2)}{1-U^2V^2},$$ which proves <a href="#eq:binary-fusion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:binary-fusion">[eq:binary-fusion]</a>. The loss is nonnegative. Under the additional bound by $\theta$, its numerator factor $2-U^2-V^2$ is at least $2(1-\theta^2)$ and its denominator is at most one. The expectation of $U^2V^2$ under the product law is $\mathbb EU^2\,\mathbb EV^2$, proving the final assertion. ◻

</details>

The subadditivity in this lemma can be iterated over the branches of a vertex. A collection of already combined signals remains independent of every unused signal conditional on the spin. Its posterior mean remains symmetric, and remains strictly inside $(-1,1)$, since $$1-\left(\frac{u+v}{1+uv}\right)^2
   =\frac{(1-u^2)(1-v^2)}{(1+uv)^2}>0
 \qquad (|u|,|v|<1).$$ Only the first two child signals below will require the sharper loss for means bounded by $\theta$.

<a id="pm-h-11"></a>
#### The amount of information in one component

For a lower bound, we use a weighted linear estimator in the tradition of second-moment reconstruction estimates on trees (Evans et al. 2000) <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[EvansKenyonPeresSchulman2000](#ref-EvansKenyonPeresSchulman2000)]; see also Mossel–Peres (Mossel and Peres 2003, sec. 5, Lemma 5.3) <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[MosselPeres2003](#ref-MosselPeres2003)]. Here it uses the Gaussian observations at all depths up to a cutoff. The cutoff balances the independent noise against the correlations among the spins. For the upper bound, the fusion loss gives the complementary estimate.

<a id="stmt-lem-critical-information"></a>
<div id="lem:critical-information" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:critical-information"></a>
**Lemma 4** (Critical posterior information). *There are constants $c,C>0$, depending only on $d$, such that for every $t>0$ and every vertex $v$, $$\label{eq:critical-information}
 c\omega(t)\le M(t)\le C\omega(t),
 \qquad
 \mathbb Em_v(t)^2\le C\omega(t).$$ One may take $c=1/5$. In particular both upper bounds are $C\sqrt t$ for $0<t\le1$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-critical-information"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-critical-information</summary>

<a id="eq:linear-signal-correlation"></a>
*Proof.* Root $T_{v\to w}$ at $v$. It is a rooted $b$-ary tree; write $|u|$ for the depth of $u$ in it. For an integer $n\ge0$, form the component-observable statistic $$Z_n=\sum_{|u|\le n}\theta^{|u|}X_u(t).$$ The broadcast covariance is $\mathbb E[\sigma_u\sigma_z]=\theta^{\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(u,z)}$. Since $b\theta^2=1$, $$\label{eq:linear-signal-correlation}
 \mathbb E[\sigma_vZ_n]
   =t\sum_{k=0}^n b^k\theta^{2k}=t(n+1).$$ The Brownian part of $Z_n$ has variance $t(n+1)$ and is independent of its spin part.

<a id="eq:linear-signal-second-moment"></a>
We count the latter part’s second moment. If an ordered pair $(u,z)$ has depths $k,\ell$ and its last common ancestor has depth $j$, its weighted spin covariance, before the factor $t^2$, is $$\theta^{k+\ell+\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(u,z)}
   =\theta^{2(k+\ell-j)}
   =b^{-(k+\ell-j)}.$$ When $j<\min(k,\ell)$, choose the common ancestor, the two distinct first children in their ordered roles, and then the remaining descendants. The exact count is $$b^j\,b(b-1)\,b^{k-j-1}b^{\ell-j-1}
       =(b-1)b^{k+\ell-j-1}.$$ When $j=\min(k,\ell)$, the shallower vertex is an ancestor of the deeper one. There are $b^{\max(k,\ell)}$ such ordered pairs; this includes the $b^k$ diagonal pairs when $k=\ell=j$. Each count is at most $b^{k+\ell-j}$. Thus the weighted contribution for each triple $(k,\ell,j)$ is at most one, and $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathbb EZ_n^2
 &\le t(n+1)+t^2
       \sum_{k,\ell=0}^n\bigl(\min(k,\ell)+1\bigr)\notag\\
 &=t(n+1)+t^2\sum_{j=0}^n(n-j+1)^2
 \le t(n+1)+t^2(n+1)^3.
 \label{eq:linear-signal-second-moment}
\end{aligned}$$ Because $Z_n$ is measurable from the component observations, $\mathbb E[\sigma_vZ_n]=\mathbb E[(\tanh Y)Z_n]$. Cauchy–Schwarz and <a href="#eq:linear-signal-correlation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:linear-signal-correlation">[eq:linear-signal-correlation]</a>–<a href="#eq:linear-signal-second-moment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:linear-signal-second-moment">[eq:linear-signal-second-moment]</a> therefore give <a id="eq:critical-information-lower"></a>$$\label{eq:critical-information-lower}
 M(t)\ge\frac{t(n+1)}{1+t(n+1)^2}.$$ For $0<t\le1$, take $n=\lfloor t^{-1/2}\rfloor$. Then $t^{-1/2}\le n+1\le2t^{-1/2}$, so the last bound is at least $\sqrt t/5$. For $t\ge1$, take $n=0$ to obtain $M(t)\ge t/(1+t)\ge1/2$. This proves the lower bound with $c=1/5$.

For the upper bound, condition on the component root spin. Its own observation history and the $b$ child-component histories are conditionally independent. The own signal has posterior mean $\tanh X_v(t)$, by the one-spin version of <a href="#eq:finite-posterior" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:finite-posterior">[eq:finite-posterior]</a>, and $$q_0(t):=\mathbb E\tanh^2X_v(t)\le\mathbb EX_v(t)^2=t+t^2.$$ One child’s entire observation history has posterior mean $\theta\tanh Y$ about the root spin: conditional on the child spin, the root has mean $\theta$ times that spin. Its second moment is $\theta^2M(t)$, and its magnitude is at most $\theta$. These statements concern full infinite component histories; their conditional independence follows from the broadcast construction and the independent Brownian noises, first for cylinder events and then for the sigma-fields they generate.

There are at least two child signals because $b\ge2$. Combine two of them first. Lemma <a href="#lem:binary-fusion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:binary-fusion">3</a> subtracts at least $2(1-\theta^2)\theta^4M(t)^2$. Add the other $b-2$ child signals and the own signal by subadditivity. The combined signal is the full component history, and hence $$M(t)\le b\theta^2M(t)
       -2(1-\theta^2)\theta^4M(t)^2+q_0(t).$$ The linear terms cancel at $b\theta^2=1$. Since $q_0(t)\le2t$ for $t\le1$, this yields $M(t)\le C\sqrt t$. At a whole vertex there are $d$ child components, so subadditivity alone gives $$\mathbb Em_v(t)^2\le q_0(t)+d\theta^2M(t)\le C\sqrt t
 \qquad(0<t\le1).$$ Both second moments are at most one for $t\ge1$, completing <a href="#eq:critical-information" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:critical-information">[eq:critical-information]</a>. ◻

</details>

We have now identified the critical information scale. To turn its lower bound into decay along a path, we need the conditional variation of a side branch’s outgoing message, even when the attaching spin has been specified.

<a id="stmt-lem-component-spin-tilts"></a>
<div id="lem:component-spin-tilts" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:component-field-tilts"></a>
**Lemma 5** (Conditioning a component on a neighboring spin). *For every integrable function $f(Y)$ and $\eta\in\{-1,1\}$, $$\label{eq:component-field-tilts}
 \begin{aligned}
 \mathbb E[f(Y)\mid\sigma_v=\eta]
   &=\mathbb E[f(Y)(1+\eta\tanh Y)],\\
 \mathbb E[f(Y)\mid\sigma_w=\eta]
   &=\mathbb E[f(Y)(1+\eta\theta\tanh Y)].
 \end{aligned}$$ In particular either conditioning leaves the expectation of every integrable even function of $Y$ unchanged.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-component-spin-tilts"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-component-spin-tilts</summary>

*Proof.* Let $\mathcal G=\mathcal F_t^{T_{v\to w}}$. Since $\sigma_v$ is a uniform sign and $\mathbb E[\sigma_v\mid\mathcal G]=\tanh Y$, conditioning the indicator of $\{\sigma_v=\eta\}$ on $\mathcal G$ proves the first identity. The component observations are independent of $\sigma_w$ conditional on $\sigma_v$, and $\mathbb E[\sigma_w\mid\sigma_v]=\theta\sigma_v$. It follows that $\mathbb E[\sigma_w\mid\mathcal G]=\theta\tanh Y$; the same indicator calculation proves the second identity. The multiplying densities are bounded, so the formulas apply to every marginally integrable $f(Y)$. If $f$ is even, its product with $\tanh Y$ is odd. Symmetry of the marginal law of $Y$ makes that correction vanish. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-12"></a>
#### Attenuation from an intact side branch

The next argument uses the full infinite component beyond one side edge. Its conditioning specifies the spins on a deterministic path, while leaving the selected side branches unobserved. This is the setting in which the information lower bound supplies a loss uniformly over the contribution from the rest of the tree.

<a id="stmt-lem-path-attenuation"></a>
<div id="lem:path-attenuation" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:shifted-attenuation"></a>
<a id="eq:conditional-path-attenuation"></a>
**Lemma 6** (Conditional path attenuation). *There is $c_0>0$, depending only on $d$, such that for every $t>0$, every $z\in\mathbb R$, and $\eta\in\{-1,1\}$, $$\label{eq:shifted-attenuation}
 \mathbb E\left[
   a\bigl(z+\psi(h_{v\to w}(t))\bigr)^2
       \,\middle|\,\sigma_w=\eta\right]
 \le1-c_0\omega(t).$$ For every deterministic simple path $v_0,\ldots,v_r$, $r\ge1$, $$\label{eq:conditional-path-attenuation}
 \mathbb E\left[
   \prod_{i=1}^r a(h_{v_i\to v_{i-1}}(t))^2
       \,\middle|\,\sigma_{v_0},\ldots,\sigma_{v_r}\right]
 \le e^{-c_0\omega(t)r}.$$ The same bound holds for the reverse product $\prod_{i=1}^r a(h_{v_{i-1}\to v_i}(t))^2$, and both bounds also hold without conditioning on the path spins.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-path-attenuation"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-path-attenuation</summary>

<a id="eq:side-message-variance"></a>
*Proof.* Set $G=\psi(Y)$ and condition on the attaching spin $\sigma_w=\eta$. We have $|G|\le\beta$. The second identity in <a href="#eq:component-field-tilts" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:component-field-tilts">[eq:component-field-tilts]</a>, symmetry, and Cauchy–Schwarz give $$\mathbb E[G^2\mid\sigma_w=\eta]=\mathbb EG^2,\qquad
 |\mathbb E[G\mid\sigma_w=\eta]|^2
   =\theta^2|\mathbb E[G\tanh Y]|^2
   \le\theta^2\mathbb EG^2.$$ Moreover $|\psi(y)|\ge\theta|\tanh y|$, since $|\operatorname{atanh}u|\ge|u|$. Consequently $$\label{eq:side-message-variance}
 \mathop{\mathrm{Var}}(G\mid\sigma_w=\eta)
   \ge(1-\theta^2)\mathbb EG^2
   \ge(1-\theta^2)\theta^2M(t).$$ The conditional variance is the useful quantity here: unlike an uncentered second moment, it cannot be eliminated by adding a shift.

<a id="eq:shifted-tanh-variance"></a>
For any random variable $G$ supported on $[-\beta,\beta]$, there is a constant $c_\beta>0$ such that $$\label{eq:shifted-tanh-variance}
 \mathbb E\tanh^2(z+G)\ge c_\beta\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}(G)
 \qquad\text{for every }z\in\mathbb R.$$ To see this, first suppose $|z|\le\beta+1$. Then $|z+G|\le2\beta+1$. The ratio $\tanh^2 u/u^2$, extended continuously to be one at $u=0$, has a positive minimum on that compact interval. Hence $\mathbb E\tanh^2(z+G)\ge c_\beta\mathbb E(z+G)^2\ge c_\beta\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}(G)$. If $|z|>\beta+1$, then $|z+G|>1$, so the left side is at least $\tanh^2(1)$; use $\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}(G)\le\beta^2$ and decrease $c_\beta$ if needed. This proves <a href="#eq:shifted-tanh-variance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:shifted-tanh-variance">[eq:shifted-tanh-variance]</a> for the conditional laws in <a href="#eq:side-message-variance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:side-message-variance">[eq:side-message-variance]</a> as well.

For $u=\tanh^2h$, the elementary bounds on $a$ give $$a(h)^2\le a(h)
   =1-\frac{(1-\theta^2)u}{1-\theta^2u}
   \le1-(1-\theta^2)u.$$ Apply this with $h=z+G$. Equations <a href="#eq:shifted-tanh-variance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:shifted-tanh-variance">[eq:shifted-tanh-variance]</a> and <a href="#eq:side-message-variance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:side-message-variance">[eq:side-message-variance]</a>, followed by $M(t)\ge\omega(t)/5$, prove <a href="#eq:shifted-attenuation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:shifted-attenuation">[eq:shifted-attenuation]</a>, for example with $$c_0=\min\left\{\frac12,\,
       \frac{c_\beta(1-\theta^2)^2\theta^2}{5}\right\}.$$ The same estimate may be used with a random shift after conditioning on data that fix the shift and are independent of the selected branch given its attaching spin.

For the path estimate, choose at each target $v_i$, using only the path geometry, one neighbor $s_i$ that is not on the path. There are $d-2\ge1$ choices at an internal target and $d-1$ at the last target. The components $R_i=T_{s_i\to v_i}$ are disjoint and infinite. In particular, when $d=3$ an internal target still has one full infinite side component.

Let $$\mathcal H=\sigma\bigl(
   \sigma_{v_0},\ldots,\sigma_{v_r},\
   X_u(s):u\notin\textstyle\bigcup_iR_i,\ 0\le s\le t\bigr).$$ Conditional on $\mathcal H$, the selected component observation arrays are independent, and the law in $R_i$ depends only on its attaching spin $\sigma_{v_i}$. This follows directly by rooting the broadcast at $v_0$: the multiplier on each attaching edge, the multipliers inside its selected component, and its Brownian motions form one independent group. The outside observations use none of these groups once the path spins are specified. The assertion for the full observation arrays follows from the corresponding assertion on finite cylinder events.

At this fixed time, the cavity identity gives, almost surely, $$h_{v_i\to v_{i-1}}
   =\psi(h_{s_i\to v_i})+
      \left(X_{v_i}
        +\sum_{\substack{u\sim v_i\\u\ne v_{i-1},\,s_i}}
                      \psi(h_{u\to v_i})\right).$$ When all other selected arrays are held fixed, the parenthesized quantity is a fixed shift independent of the array in $R_i$ conditional on $\sigma_{v_i}$. Integrate $R_1$ first. Every factor at a later target $v_j$, $j>1$, uses its component on the side excluding $v_{j-1}$, and hence does not use $R_1$. Thus <a href="#eq:shifted-attenuation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:shifted-attenuation">[eq:shifted-attenuation]</a> removes the first squared factor at a cost at most $1-c_0\omega(t)$. Repeating in the order $R_2,\ldots,R_r$ gives $$\mathbb E\left[\prod_{i=1}^r a(h_{v_i\to v_{i-1}})^2
       \,\middle|\,\mathcal H\right]
 \le (1-c_0\omega(t))^r
 \le e^{-c_0\omega(t)r}.$$ Taking a conditional expectation given only the path spins proves <a href="#eq:conditional-path-attenuation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:conditional-path-attenuation">[eq:conditional-path-attenuation]</a>. Reversing the deterministic path gives the stated reverse product. Finally average over its spins. ◻

</details>

The loss above comes from the posterior information in an intact infinite branch. It is not a claim about arbitrarily truncated branches: a finite boundary will require a separate estimate using the target’s own Gaussian observation. For the present response row, the infinite-branch estimate is exactly what is needed.

<a id="pm-h-13"></a>
#### Time-integrable response rows

Return to the full range $0<\theta\le b^{-1/2}$. For the progressive coefficients in <a href="#eq:covariance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:covariance">[eq:covariance]</a>, define the extended nonnegative sum $$A_v(t)=\sum_{u\in\mathcal V}c_{vu}(t)^2.$$ It is measurable as a countable sum. The following estimate is the input that permits division by the posterior variance when the filtering equation is converted to a field equation.

<a id="stmt-prop-row-energy"></a>
<div id="prop:row-energy" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:row-energy"></a>
**Proposition 7** (Time-integrable response rows). *For every vertex $v$, there is $C<\infty$, depending only on $d$ at the critical parameter, such that $$\label{eq:row-energy}
 \begin{aligned}
 A_v(t)&\le1+\frac{d\theta^2}{1-b\theta^2}
      &&\text{if }b\theta^2<1,\quad t\ge0,\\
 \mathbb EA_v(t)&\le\frac{C}{\omega(t)}
      &&\text{if }b\theta^2=1,\quad t>0,\\
 \mathbb E\int_0^T A_v(t)\,\mathrm dt&<\infty
      &&\text{for every }T<\infty\text{ in either case}.
 \end{aligned}$$ The same fixed-time and integrated upper bounds hold with $\sum_u C_{vu}(t)^2$ in place of $A_v(t)$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-row-energy"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-row-energy</summary>

*Proof.* There are $db^{r-1}$ vertices at distance $r\ge1$ from $v$. If $b\theta^2<1$, the pointwise bound $a\le1$ gives $$A_v(t)\le1+\sum_{r\ge1}db^{r-1}\theta^{2r}
       =1+\frac{d\theta^2}{1-b\theta^2}.$$ At equality, $db^{r-1}\theta^{2r}=d/b$. Tonelli’s theorem and Lemma <a href="#lem:path-attenuation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:path-attenuation">6</a> give, for $t>0$, $$\mathbb EA_v(t)
  \le1+\frac db\sum_{r\ge1}e^{-c_0\omega(t)r}
  =1+\frac{d/b}{e^{c_0\omega(t)}-1}
  \le1+\frac{d}{bc_0\omega(t)}
  \le\frac{C}{\omega(t)}.$$ Here $e^x-1\ge x$ and $\omega(t)\le1$. Since $$\int_0^T\frac{\,\mathrm dt}{\omega(t)}
   =
   \begin{cases}
     2\sqrt T,&0\le T\le1,\\
     T+1,&T\ge1,
   \end{cases}$$ another application of Tonelli proves the integrated bound. Finally $\sum_u C_{vu}^2=(1-m_v^2)^2A_v\le A_v$ by <a href="#eq:covariance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:covariance">[eq:covariance]</a>. ◻

</details>

In particular, $A_v(t)$ is finite almost surely at each fixed positive time and is finite for almost every time almost surely on each compact horizon. At criticality the raw sum at $t=0$ is infinite: all fields vanish, $a(0)=1$, and every positive-distance shell contributes $d/b$. That single time has zero Lebesgue measure. The integrated estimate, rather than a bound at time zero, is the conclusion used in the construction of the infinite innovation equation.

<a id="sec:innovations"></a>
### Constructing the infinite innovation equation

The posterior fields describe how the observations affect the spins. We now turn that description into a stochastic equation driven by independent Brownian motions. This is the equation that will allow us to compare two observation processes driven by the same noise. Throughout the section, $$b=d-1,\qquad 0<\theta=\tanh\beta\le b^{-1/2}.$$ We use the raw observation versions from Lemma <a href="#lem:posterior" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:posterior">2</a>. In particular, at this stage a posterior identity holds almost surely at each fixed time, and therefore outside a $\,\mathrm dt\,\,\mathrm d\mathbb P$-null set; continuity of the infinite filter has not yet been established.

<a id="eq:innovations-definition"></a>
For each vertex define its *innovation* by $$\label{eq:innovations-definition}
 W_v(t)=X_v(t)-\int_0^t m_v(s)\,\mathrm ds.$$ The integrand is a bounded, progressively measurable observation function. Thus this formula defines continuous paths for every observation input. Their values through time $t$ use only the observation history through $t$. The time integrals are Borel functions of that history, so $X\mapsto W$ is a causal Borel map on the countable product of continuous path spaces. The centered-ball definitions of the posterior means also show that this map commutes with graph relabeling.

The innovation construction is classical in nonlinear filtering (Fujisaki, Kallianpur, and Kunita 1972) <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[FujisakiKallianpurKunita1972](#ref-FujisakiKallianpurKunita1972)] and is the starting point of the Brownian Ising construction of Nam, Sly, and Zhang (Nam, Sly, and Zhang 2022) <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[NSZ](#ref-NSZ)]. Here we must justify its equation for countably many observations. We will first prove that $W$ is Brownian, then identify the entire posterior martingale using an exact energy limit. Neither step assumes that $W$ determines $X$.

<a id="stmt-prop-innovations"></a>
<div id="prop:innovations" class="proposition">

**Proposition 8** (Infinite innovation equations). *In the planted observation law $X_v(t)=t\sigma_v+B_v(t)$, every finite subvector of $(W_v)_{v\in\mathcal V}$ is a standard vector Brownian motion in both the raw observation filtration $(\mathcal F_t^0)$ and its usual augmentation $(\mathcal F_t)$. In particular $W$ has product Wiener law.*

<a id="eq:magnetization-sde"></a>
